<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Revised 2022 Jan 12; Received 2021 Nov 15; Accepted 2022 Jan 17; Collection date 2022 Jun 1.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2022 The Authors. <em>Human Brain Mapping</em> published by Wiley Periodicals LLC.</div>
<p>This is an open access article under the terms of the <a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-nc/4.0/</a> License, which permits use, distribution and reproduction in any medium, provided the original work is properly cited and is not used for commercial purposes.</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC9057096  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35122347/" class="usa-link">35122347</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>The visual attentional blink can be substantially reduced by delivering a task‐irrelevant sound synchronously with the second visual target (T2), and this effect is further modulated by the semantic congruency between the sound and T2. However, whether the cross‐modal benefit originates from audiovisual interactions or sound‐induced alertness remains controversial, and whether the semantic congruency effect is contingent on audiovisual temporal synchrony needs further investigation. The current study investigated these questions by recording event‐related potentials (ERPs) in a visual attentional blink task wherein a sound could either synchronize with T2, precede T2 by 200 ms, be delayed by 100 ms, or be absent, and could be either semantically congruent or incongruent with T2 when delivered. The behavioral data showed that both the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination and the further semantic modulation were the largest when the sound synchronized with T2. In parallel, the ERP data yielded that both the early occipital cross‐modal P195 component (192–228 ms after T2 onset) and late parietal cross‐modal N440 component (424–448 ms) were prominent only when the sound synchronized with T2, with the former being elicited solely when the sound was further semantically congruent whereas the latter occurring only when that sound was incongruent. These findings demonstrate not only that the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink stems from early audiovisual interactions and the semantic congruency effect depends on audiovisual temporal synchrony, but also that the semantic modulation can unfold at the early stage of visual discrimination processing.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> attentional blink, audiovisual, cross‐modal interaction, ERPs, semantic congruency, temporal synchrony</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract2"><hr class="headless">
<p>The cross‐modal boost of target discrimination during the attentional blink and the further semantic modulation were the largest when the sound synchronized with the target. The cross‐modal boost of target discrimination during the attentional blink stems from early audiovisual interactions and the semantic congruency effect depends on audiovisual temporal synchrony.</p>
<section class="bt xbox font-sm" id="hbm25797-blkfxd-0001"><p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/57aa/9057096/df68a9379e17/HBM-43-2478-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-1" height="150" width="195" alt="graphic file with name HBM-43-2478-g007.jpg"></p></section></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0001"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. INTRODUCTION</h2>
<p>Our ability to precisely extract important visual information from a rapidly changing environment is rather limited. One of the most striking examples illustrating this temporal limitation of attention is the attentional blink phenomenon (Raymond, Shapiro, &amp; Arnell, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0054" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0054">1992</a>)—if two successive visual targets are embedded in a rapid serial visual presentation (RSVP) stream, observers often fail to discriminate the second target (T2) when it appears 200–300 ms after the first target (T1). However, recent studies have consistently shown that a task‐irrelevant, meaningless auditory stimulus delivered synchronously with T2 could substantially boost T2 discrimination during the attentional blink interval (Kranczioch &amp; Thorne, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0029">2013</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>; Olivers &amp; Van der Burg, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0052" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0052">2008</a>), indicating information from the auditory modality can help to overcome the temporal limitation of visual attention to some extent. Moreover, using event‐related potential (ERP) recordings and line drawings of common objects (e.g., dogs) paired with semantically congruent or incongruent sounds (e.g., barks of dogs or beeps of cars), a more recent study explored the neural substrates of this cross‐modal facilitation and the possible audiovisual semantic congruency effect on it (Zhao, Feng, Huang, Wang, &amp; Feng, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>). It was found that the semantically congruent sounds induced a larger T2 accuracy enhancement than the incongruent sounds, and the behavioral improvements induced by both the congruent and incongruent sounds were associated with a visual N1‐like early ERP component (~200 ms after T2 onset) over the occipital region, whereas the lower T2 accuracy for the incongruent sounds was correlated with an N400‐like late ERP component (~400 ms) over the parietal scalp (Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>). These findings demonstrate that the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink has an early neural processing locus while the modulation of audiovisual semantic congruency occurs at a relatively late stage of processing.</p>
<p>Nevertheless, there is still an unresolved debate regarding whether the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink originates from genuine audiovisual cross‐modal interactions or is merely a manifestation of sound‐induced, modality‐nonspecific alerting effect (Kranczioch &amp; Thorne, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0029">2013</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>; Olivers &amp; Van der Burg, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0052" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0052">2008</a>). The pioneering investigation showed that the cross‐modal boost effect (i.e., the sound‐induced T2 accuracy enhancement relative to the no‐sound condition) occurred when the sound synchronized with T2 but not when delivered 250 ms before T2 onset (Olivers &amp; Van der Burg, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0052" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0052">2008</a>). These findings were considered as in favor of the cross‐modal interaction hypothesis, because the efficiency of cross‐modal interaction generally decreases as the audiovisual temporal asynchrony increases (Meredith, Nemitz, &amp; Stein, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0043" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0043">1987</a>; Spence, Shore, &amp; Klein, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0059" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0059">2001</a>; Stone et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0061" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0061">2001</a>; van Wassenhove, Grant, &amp; Poeppel, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0069">2007</a>; Zampini, Guest, Shore, &amp; Spence, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0073" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0073">2005</a>), whereas the alerting effect is typically maximal when the alerting stimulus is presented 100–300 ms prior to a target stimulus (Bertelson, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0004" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0004">1967</a>; Los &amp; Van den Heuvel, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0036" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0036">2001</a>; Niemi &amp; Näätänen, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0050" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0050">1981</a>; Posner &amp; Boies, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0053" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0053">1971</a>). In contrast, subsequent researchers found that although the cross‐modal boost effect was not greater when the sound preceded T2 by 250 ms than when it synchronized with T2, the cross‐modal boost effect in the preceding‐sound condition was indeed significant (relative to the no‐sound condition) rather than absent (Kranczioch &amp; Thorne, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0029">2013</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>). Their results seem to suggest that the alerting hypothesis could still account for the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink to some degree.</p>
<p>The controversial findings regarding the psychological mechanisms of the cross‐modal boost effect may be attributed to different experimental designs used in previous studies. First, since the probability for the synchronous‐sound condition was four times higher than the preceding‐sound condition in Olivers and Van der Burg's (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0052" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0052">2008</a>) study (see their Exp. 4), it cannot rule out the possibility that low probability for the preceding‐sound condition might have attenuated the potential sound‐induced alerting effect. Second, given that the preceding‐sound and synchronous‐sound conditions were presented in <em>separate</em> blocks (sessions) in studies of Kranczioch and Thorne (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0029">2013</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>), the sound would always precede T2 when delivered in a preceding‐sound block. Previous studies have shown that the human brain can make rapid recalibration to repeatedly presented, temporally asynchronous audiovisual stimuli and increase the probability of audiovisual integration (Bhat, Miller, Pitt, &amp; Shahin, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0006" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0006">2015</a>; Fujisaki, Shimojo, Kashino, &amp; Nishida, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0018" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0018">2004</a>; Simon, Noel, &amp; Wallace, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0057" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0057">2017</a>; Van der Burg, Alais, &amp; Cass, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0066" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0066">2013</a>). Based on these prior findings, it is possible that audiovisual temporal recalibration may have occurred in response to T2 and the 100% preceding sound (when delivered) in the preceding‐sound blocks of Kranczioch and Thorne (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0029">2013</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>), leading to audiovisual integration. Thus, the origin of the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink is still equivocal and needs to be determined with improvements in the experimental paradigm.</p>
<p>Furthermore, it also deserves further investigation concerning whether the effect of higher‐order audiovisual semantic congruency on the visual attentional blink depends on audiovisual temporal synchrony. Although a recent study has found that a semantically congruent sound led to higher T2 discrimination accuracy than a semantically incongruent sound even when these sounds preceded T2 by 210 ms (Adam &amp; Noppeney, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0001">2014</a>), it should be noted again that similar to Kranczioch and Thorne's (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0029" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0029">2013</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>) studies, the preceding‐sound and synchronous‐sound conditions were also presented in <em>separate</em> blocks in the study of Adam and Noppeney (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0001">2014</a>). Meanwhile, there was no sound‐absent condition in the study. Accordingly, in their preceding‐sound blocks, although the sound could be either semantically congruent or incongruent with T2, it preceded T2 on <em>each</em> trial. As illustrated above, this kind of block‐design may have triggered the audiovisual temporal recalibration in response to T2 and the 100% preceding sound (Bhat et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0006" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0006">2015</a>; Fujisaki et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0018" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0018">2004</a>; Simon et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0057" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0057">2017</a>; Van der Burg et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0066" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0066">2013</a>), thereby weakening the potential effect of audiovisual temporal synchrony. Hence, the existing evidence seems insufficient to answer whether the audiovisual semantic congruency effect on the visual attentional blink is genuinely independent of audiovisual temporal synchrony.</p>
<p>The current study investigated the questions mentioned above in an extended version of the visual attentional blink paradigm recently described by Zhao et al. (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>), under which a task‐irrelevant but natural sound could synchronize with T2, precede T2 by 200 ms, be delayed relative to T2 by 100 ms or be absent, and could be either semantically congruent or incongruent with T2 when delivered (e.g., a bark of a dog with a drawing of a dog, or a beep of a car with a drawing of a dog). Notably, the temporal position of the sound was manipulated <em>within</em> each block and different sound temporal positions were kept <em>equally</em> probable in the present study. Accordingly, if the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink originates from audiovisual cross‐modal interactions and the audiovisual semantic congruency effect is contingent on audiovisual temporal synchrony, the present study should predict: (a) the cross‐modal boost effect would be the largest when the sound synchronized with T2 and would be prominent but with a decreased magnitude when the sound was delayed by 100 ms, because a 100‐ms audiovisual asynchrony is still within the temporal window of integration (Donohue, Roberts, Grent‐'t‐Jong, &amp; Woldorff, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0014" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0014">2011</a>; Meredith et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0043" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0043">1987</a>; Spence et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0059" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0059">2001</a>; van Wassenhove et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0069" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0069">2007</a>; Zampini et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0073" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0073">2005</a>); (b) the audiovisual semantic congruency effect on the cross‐modal boost would be the greatest when the congruent and incongruent sounds synchronized with T2. Importantly, in order to provide electrophysiological evidence for the present behavioral findings, high time‐resolution ERP data were recorded concurrently with the behavioral task here, and ERP components that have been shown to underlie the cross‐modal boost effect and the audiovisual semantic congruency effect (Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>) were analyzed as functions of audiovisual temporal synchrony and semantic congruency.</p></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0002"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. METHODS</h2>
<section id="hbm25797-sec-0003"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Participants</h3>
<p>The pilot behavioral experiment (<em>n</em> = 20) showed that the 3 (sound temporal position: precede, synchronize, delay) × 2 (semantic congruency: congruent, incongruent) repeated‐measures ANOVA on the cross‐modal boost effect had a significant two‐way interaction, with its effect size <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> being equal to 0.154. Thus, given an alpha level of .05, at least 28 subjects were required to achieve a power of 0.8 when focusing on the two‐way interaction, which was computed using MorePower 6.0.4 (Campbell &amp; Thompson, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0011" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0011">2012</a>). To obtain reliable results that were comparable to those reported in the study of Zhao et al. (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>) (<em>n</em> = 34) wherein the same stimuli and a similar experimental design were used, the current electroencephalogram (EEG) experiment recruited 42 healthy subjects (30 female and 12 males; age range of 18–28 years, mean age of 20.9 years; all right‐handed). All subjects verbally reported normal or corrected‐to‐normal vision as well as normal audition, and could easily recognize the object category (dog, car, drum) of all visual and auditory stimuli used in the experiment, although more standardized tests for assessing visual and auditory functions should be adopted in future studies. They were naive as to the hypothesis of the experiment. In accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki, written informed consent as approved by the Human Research Protections Program of Soochow University was obtained from all subjects before their participation.</p></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0004"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Apparatus, stimuli and design</h3>
<p>The experiment was performed in a dark and sound‐attenuated room. Stimulus presentation was scripted using “Presentation” software (version 18.0, NeuroBehavioral Systems, Inc.). Visual stimuli were presented on a 27‐in. LCD monitor (ASUS PG279Q, resolution 1,920 × 1,080, refresh rate 120 Hz) on which the background color was set to gray. Auditory stimuli were delivered by a pair of loudspeakers (HiVi X3) positioned on the left and right sides of the monitor symmetrically, so that a single sound presented by the two speakers simultaneously would be perceived as coming from the center of the monitor (Bertelson &amp; Aschersleben, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0005" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0005">1998</a>). Subjects sat in front of the monitor with a viewing distance of approximately 80 cm, and were required to maintain their eyes fixated on a red cross (0.3° × 0.3°), which was displayed at the center of the screen throughout each RSVP stream. The visual stimuli consisted of 48 black‐and‐white line drawings (each 5.6° × 4.5°), including 30 unique drawings of houses used as distractors, nine unique drawings (three clothes, three cups, and three flowers) used as the first target (T1), and the remaining nine unique drawings (three dogs, three cars and three drums) used as the second target (T2). The line drawings for T1 and T2 were from two non‐overlapping sets in order to avoid priming (Koelewijn, Van der Burg, Bronkhorst, &amp; Theeuwes, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0027" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0027">2008</a>) or repetition blindness effects (Kanwisher, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0023" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0023">1987</a>). The auditory stimuli were comprised of nine unique natural sounds (three barks of dogs, three beeps of cars, and three beats of drums; all stereo) that were 200 ms in duration (with 20 ms rise and fall ramps) and approximately 75 dB in loudness at subjects' ears when delivered. These line drawings and natural sounds were all taken from the study of Zhao et al. (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>) wherein a similar basic experimental design was used.</p>
<p>The whole experiment consisted of 27 blocks of 60 trials each, resulting in a total of 1,620 trials, which were performed by each participant. Each trial began with the presentation of the red fixation for a fixed period of 1,000 ms, immediately followed by an RSVP stream presented at the center of the screen (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1a</a>). Each RSVP stream was comprised of 17 distinct line drawings, including T1, T2 and 15 distractors. The distractors for each trial were sampled randomly (without repetition) from the aforementioned 30 drawings of houses. Each drawing in the RSVP stream was presented immediately after the offset of the preceding drawing, and the duration of each drawing was 100 ms [i.e., the drawing‐to‐drawing stimulus onset asynchrony (SOA) was 100 ms]. T1 could be one of the nine drawings (three clothes, three cups and three flowers; Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1a</a>, right) with equal probability, and was presented randomly from the third to the fifth position in the RSVP stream. T2 could be one of the remaining nine drawings (three dogs, three cars and three drums; Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1a</a>, bottom right) equiprobably, with its presented position in the RSVP stream varying with different experimental conditions, listed below.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="hbm25797-fig-0001"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/57aa/9057096/6ccd5d71daee/HBM-43-2478-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-3" height="540" width="709" alt="FIGURE 1"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/hbm25797-fig-0001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(a) Schematic illustration of the RSVP stream exemplified for a lag 3 trial on which T2 was presented 3 positions after T1, together with a semantically congruent but task‐irrelevant sound that could synchronize with T2, precede T2 by 200 ms, or be delayed relative to T2 by 100 ms. The task for participants was to discriminate sequentially the exact identities of T1 and T2 without time limit after each RSVP stream, while ignoring all sounds. Note that the optional drawings for T1 and T2 and their corresponding button numbers were presented to the participants when they responded, as shown on the right side. (b) Detailed illustration of the RSVP sequences in all 12 experimental conditions (see section <a href="#hbm25797-sec-0004" class="usa-link">2.2</a>). The probability for each condition was 1/12, and ERP waveforms in all but V_lag8 condition were time‐locked to the onset of visual stimulus at lag 3 position for further analysis</p></figcaption></figure><p>On 7/12 of all trials, T2 was presented three positions after T1 (i.e., at lag 3, T1‐to‐T2 SOA of 300 ms). Specifically, on 6/7 of these lag 3 trials (i.e., 6/12 of all trials), a task‐irrelevant natural sound [i.e., one of the nine unique sounds (three barks of dogs, three beeps of cars, and three beats of drums) with equal probability] could either synchronize with T2 (labeled as <em>sync</em>, 2/12 of all trials), or precede T2 by 200 ms (labeled as <em>prec</em>, 2/12 of all trials), or be delayed relative to T2 by 100 ms (labeled as <em>delay</em>, 2/12 of all trials; see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1a</a>). Meanwhile, the sound, regardless of its onset moment, could be either semantically congruent with T2 [labeled as <em>VAcon</em> (audiovisual congruent), 3/12 of all trials; e.g., a bark of a dog with a drawing of a dog] or semantically incongruent with T2 [labeled as <em>VAincon</em> (audiovisual incongruent), 3/12 of all trials; e.g., a beep of a car with a drawing of a dog]. On the remaining 1/7 of the lag 3 trials (i.e., 1/12 of all trials), T2 was presented without any nearing sound [labeled as <em>V</em> (visual‐only)]. Accordingly, there were seven resulting experimental conditions for the lag 3 trials, namely, <em>V (V_lag3)</em>, <em>VAcon_prec</em>, <em>VAcon_sync</em>, <em>VAcon_delay</em>, <em>VAincon_prec</em>, <em>VAincon_sync</em> and <em>VAincon_delay</em> (see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1b</a> for full comprehension).</p>
<p>On another 4/12 of all trials, a white rectangle with the same size of line drawings (i.e., a blank drawing) was presented at lag 3 position. On 3/4 of these trials (i.e., 3/12 of all trials), a random one of the nine natural sounds could either synchronize with the blank drawing at lag 3 [labeled as <em>A_sync</em> (auditory‐only_synchronize), 1/12 of all trials], or precede the blank drawing by 200 ms [labeled as <em>A_prec</em> (auditory‐only_precede), 1/12 of all trials], or be delayed relative to the blank drawing by 100 ms [labeled as <em>A_delay</em> (auditory‐only_delay), 1/12 of all trials; see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1b</a>]. On the remaining 1/4 of these trials (i.e., 1/12 of all trials), no sound was delivered near the blank drawing [labeled as <em>N</em> (no stimulus); see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1b</a>]. These four conditions (i.e., A_prec, A_sync, A_delay and <em>N</em>) were included in the experiment in order to isolate audiovisual cross‐modal ERP components on the lag 3 trials when the sound preceded T2, synchronized with T2, and was delayed relative to T2, respectively (see section <a href="#hbm25797-sec-0006" class="usa-link">2.4</a>). Meanwhile, in order to generate a considerable number of lag 8 trials in terms of behavioral task, T2 was then presented at lag 8 position in these conditions. Importantly, since T2 appeared 500 ms after the blank drawing onset in these conditions, it could be ensured that subjects would engage in the task without response‐related brain activity during the subsequent 500 ms interval after the blank drawing onset, which was the epoch of interest in ERP analysis. In addition, similar to the aforementioned VA conditions, in A_prec, A_sync and A_delay conditions it was equally probable whether the sound nearing the blank drawing at lag 3 was semantically congruent or incongruent with the subsequent T2 at lag 8.</p>
<p>On the remaining 1/12 of all trials, T2 was presented at lag 8, a distractor instead of a blank drawing was presented at lag 3, and no sound was delivered. Thus, this type of trials was the standard visual‐only lag 8 trials (labeled as <em>V_lag8</em>; see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1b</a>), which was included in order to check whether the basic visual attentional blink effect (i.e., much lower T2 discrimination accuracy in V_lag3 than V_lag8 condition) was successfully induced in the present study.</p>
<p>Accordingly, from the perspective of the behavioral task, there were seven types of lag 3 trials (V_lag3, VAcon_prec, VAcon_sync, VAcon_delay, VAincon_prec, VAincon_sync and VAincon_delay) and five types of lag 8 trials (A_prec, A_sync, A_delay, N and V_lag8). All these types of trials were presented in a pseudo‐randomized order with equal probability [i.e., each 1/12 (135 trials)]. It is noteworthy that the experiment did not introduce the corresponding VA, A and N conditions for the standard V_lag8 condition because of the followings: (a) the focus of the present study was the effect of sound on T2 discrimination <em>during</em> the attentional blink (i.e., at lag 3) rather than <em>outside</em> the attentional blink (i.e., at lag 8); (b) the present design allowed the collection of as many lag 3 trials as possible without increasing the experiment duration, which could minimize the fatigue effect (cf., Maier &amp; Rahman, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0040">2018</a>); (c) the study of Zhao et al. (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>) has shown that presenting a sound simultaneously with T2 at lag 8 had no effect on T2 discrimination.</p>
<p>The task for participants was to discriminate sequentially, after each RSVP stream, the exact identities of both T1 and T2 as accurately as possible in an unspeeded manner by pressing buttons on a keyboard's number pad (1–9 for T1, 1–9 for T2; see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1a</a>, right side) with the right hand, while ignoring all sounds if delivered. Note that the optional drawings for T1 and T2 (each 5.6° × 4.5°) and their corresponding button numbers were presented to the participants when they made their responses. Only if the exact identity of T1 or T2 was correctly discriminated would this discrimination be coded as a correct response. Importantly, in order to prevent subjects from responding based on sounds, subjects were informed that the identity of the sound was uninformative of the exact identity of T2, even in VAcon conditions (e.g., any single bark of dog was likely to be presented near any single drawing of dog). It was checked that the participants believed that the sounds provided no information on T2 identity (see Section B of Data <a href="#hbm25797-supitem-0001" class="usa-link">S1</a>, Supporting Information). The button‐press for T2 then triggered the next trial. Participants were encouraged to have a rest between blocks in order to relieve fatigue.</p></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0005"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Electrophysiological recording and preprocessing</h3>
<p>The electroencephalogram (EEG) was recorded continuously when subjects performed the behavioral task, using a SynAmps2 amplifier (NeuroScan, Inc.) and a custom‐built 64‐electrode elastic cap. The electrodes on the cap [FPz, FP1, FP2, AF3, AF4, Fz, F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F6, F7, F8, FCz, FC1, FC2, FC3, FC4, FC5, FC6, Cz, C1, C2, C3, C4, C5, C6, T7, T8, CPz, CP1, CP2, CP3, CP4, CP5, CP6, TP7, TP8, Pz, P1, P2, P3, P4, P5, P6, P7, P8, POz, PO3, PO4, PO5, PO6, PO7, PO8, Oz, O1, O2, Iz, I3, I4, I5 (P9), I6 (P10) and M2 (right mastoid)] were positioned according to a modified 10–10 system montage (McDonald, Teder‐Sälejärvi, Di Russo, &amp; Hillyard, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0042" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0042">2003</a>). Two additional electrodes, AFz and M1 (left mastoid), served as the ground and reference electrodes during data acquisition, respectively. Horizontal eye movements were detected by a pair of bipolar electrodes positioned at the left and right outer canthi (horizontal electrooculogram, HEOG). Vertical eye movements and blinks were detected by another pair of bipolar electrodes placed above and below the left eye (vertical electrooculogram, VEOG). The impedances of all electrodes were kept below 5 kΩ. The online EEG and EOG signals were filtered by a band‐pass filter of 0.05–100 Hz and digitized at a sampling rate of 1,000 Hz. The EEG recording was carried out using “Scan” software (version 4.5, NeuroScan, Inc.).</p>
<p>In offline preprocessing, the continuous EEG signals were firstly down‐sampled to 500 Hz, and then low‐pass filtered (half‐amplitude cutoff = 33.75 Hz, transition band width = 7.5 Hz) using a zero‐phase shifted (two‐pass forward and reverse), Hamming‐windowed sinc FIR filter to attenuate high‐frequency noise triggered by muscle activities or external electrical sources. The filtered EEG data were re‐referenced to the average of the left and right mastoid (M1 and M2) electrodes. The re‐referenced EEG signals in all conditions except the V_lag8 condition were then segmented into 600‐ms epochs time‐locked to the lag 3 position (for V_lag3, VAcon and VAincon conditions, time‐locked to T2 onset; for A and N conditions, time‐locked to the blank drawing onset; see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1b</a>) with a 100‐ms pre‐lag3 baseline and were baseline‐corrected. Automatic artifact rejection was performed based on a threshold of ±75 μV for both EEG and EOG electrodes, in order to discard epochs contaminated by eye movements, eye blinks or muscle activities. Based on previous EEG studies on the attentional blink (e.g., Haroush, Deouell, &amp; Hochstein, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0021" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0021">2011</a>; Kranczioch, Debener, Maye, &amp; Engel, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0028" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0028">2007</a>; Kranczioch &amp; Thorne, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>; Maier &amp; Rahman, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0040">2018</a>; Vogel, Luck, &amp; Shapiro, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0070">1998</a>; Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>), only trials (epochs) on which T1 identity was correctly discriminated were further analyzed, hence leaving on average 106.7 (range 69–133) valid epochs per condition (see Table <a href="#hbm25797-supitem-0001" class="usa-link">S1</a> for the average, minimal and maximal number of valid epochs in each condition). The remaining valid epochs in each condition were then averaged separately to obtain corresponding ERP waveforms. The EEG preprocessing and subsequent ERP analysis were performed using the EEGLAB toolbox (Delorme &amp; Makeig, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0013" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0013">2004</a>) in combination with custom‐built MATLAB scripts (The MathWorks, Inc.).</p></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0006"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Data analysis</h3>
<p>To reveal neural activities underlying the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink and examine the roles of audiovisual temporal synchrony and semantic congruency, the present study isolated audiovisual cross‐modal ERP components on the lag 3 trials when a semantically congruent or incongruent sound preceded T2, synchronized with T2, and was delayed relative to T2, respectively, by calculating cross‐modal difference waveforms. The cross‐modal difference waveforms were obtained by subtracting the summed ERPs elicited by the unimodal V and A stimuli (V + A) from ERPs elicited by the bimodal VA stimuli (VA), and statistically significant positive or negative waves (as compared with 0) in the difference waveforms have been considered as neural activities associated with audiovisual cross‐modal interactions (Bonath et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0007" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0007">2007</a>; Gao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0019" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0019">2014</a>; Giard &amp; Peronnet, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0020" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0020">1999</a>; Kranczioch &amp; Thorne, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>; Mishra, Martínez, &amp; Hillyard, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0044" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0044">2008</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0045" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0045">2010</a>; Mishra, Martínez, Sejnowski, &amp; Hillyard, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0046" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0046">2007</a>; Molholm et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0049" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0049">2002</a>; Molholm, Ritter, Javitt, &amp; Foxe, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0048" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0048">2004</a>; Talsma, Doty, &amp; Woldorff, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0062" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0062">2007</a>; Talsma &amp; Woldorff, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0063" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0063">2005</a>; Teder‐Sälejärvi, Di Russo, McDonald, &amp; Hillyard, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0064" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0064">2005</a>; Teder‐Sälejärvi, McDonald, Di Russo, &amp; Hillyard, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0065" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0065">2002</a>; Van der Burg, Talsma, Olivers, Hickey, &amp; Theeuwes, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0068" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0068">2011</a>; Yang et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0071" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0071">2013</a>; Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>; Zhao, Wang, Feng, &amp; Feng, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0075" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0075">2020</a>; Zhao, Wang, Xu, Feng, &amp; Feng, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0076" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0076">2018</a>). In the present study, the cross‐modal difference waveforms for different bimodal VA stimuli were calculated as follows:</p>
<ol id="hbm25797-list-0001" class="list" style="list-style-type:decimal">
<li id="hbm25797-li-0001"><p>When a semantically congruent sound preceded T2 by 200 ms: VAcon_prec − (V + A_prec).</p></li>
<li id="hbm25797-li-0002"><p>When a semantically incongruent sound preceded T2 by 200 ms: VAincon_prec − (V + A_prec).</p></li>
<li id="hbm25797-li-0003"><p>When a semantically congruent sound synchronized with T2: VAcon_sync − (V + A_sync).</p></li>
<li id="hbm25797-li-0004"><p>When a semantically incongruent sound synchronized with T2: VAincon_sync − (V + A_sync).</p></li>
<li id="hbm25797-li-0005"><p>When a semantically congruent sound was delayed relative to T2 by 100 ms: VAcon_delay − (V + A_delay).</p></li>
<li id="hbm25797-li-0006"><p>When a semantically incongruent sound was delayed relative to T2 by 100 ms: VAincon_delay − (V + A_delay).</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Prior to the aforementioned calculations, the time‐locked ERPs in N condition (wherein a blank drawing was presented at lag 3 without any sound; see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1b</a>) were first subtracted from ERPs in each of the remaining ten conditions (i.e., V_lag3, VAcon_prec, VAcon_sync, VAcon_delay, VAincon_prec, VAincon_sync, VAincon_delay, A_prec, A_sync and A_delay), in order to cancel out not only any common pre‐lag3 anticipatory activities that might extend into the post‐lag3 period and lead to false discoveries of early cross‐modal interactions (cf., Bonath et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0007" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0007">2007</a>; Mishra et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0046" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0046">2007</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0044" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0044">2008</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0045" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0045">2010</a>; Talsma &amp; Woldorff, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0063" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0063">2005</a>; Van der Burg et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0068" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0068">2011</a>; Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0076" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0076">2018</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0075" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0075">2020</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>), but also the systematic ERPs elicited by the pre‐ and post‐lag3 distractors (cf., Kranczioch &amp; Thorne, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0030" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0030">2015</a>; Luo et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0039" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0039">2013</a>; Luo, Feng, He, Wang, &amp; Luo, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0038" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0038">2010</a>; Maier &amp; Rahman, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0040">2018</a>; Sergent, Baillet, &amp; Dehaene, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0056" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0056">2005</a>; Vogel et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0070" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0070">1998</a>; Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>). In other words, the time‐locked ERPs in N condition were used as a combined estimation of not only the distractor‐elicited ERPs but also the prestimulus anticipatory ERPs.</p>
<p>In order to avoid the problem of multiple implicit comparisons that could inflate the Type I error rate (Luck &amp; Gaspelin, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0037" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0037">2017</a>), the time windows and electrodes for measuring cross‐modal ERP components associated with the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink were selected a priori in the current study based on the recent study conducted by Zhao et al. (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>) wherein the same stimuli and a similar experimental design were used. Specifically, the occipitally distributed <strong>N195</strong> component was measured as mean amplitude during the time window of 192–228 ms after T2 onset in the cross‐modal difference waveform at the electrode O1, and the parietally distributed <strong>N440</strong> component was quantified as mean amplitude within the time interval of 424–448 ms in the cross‐modal difference waveform at the electrode P1.</p>
<p>For statistical analysis, separate two‐way repeated‐measures ANOVAs with factors of sound temporal position (precede, synchronize, delay) and semantic congruency (congruent, incongruent) were first conducted on the mean amplitudes of each ERP component in the cross‐modal difference waveforms, in order to examine the influences of audiovisual temporal synchrony and semantic congruency on the neural basis underlying the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink. When there was a main effect or interaction violating the sphericity assumption, the corresponding <em>p</em> value was corrected using the Greenhouse–Geisser method. Only when the two‐way interaction was significant would it be further analyzed in the following two directions in order to better understand its pattern: (a) one‐way repeated‐measures ANOVAs with a factor of sound temporal position were conducted separately for semantically congruent and incongruent conditions, and pairwise comparisons by paired <em>t</em>‐tests between ERP amplitude when sound synchronized versus that when sound preceded, and between ERP amplitude when sound synchronized versus that when sound was delayed, were performed only after finding a significant (i.e., <em>p</em> &lt; .05) main effect of sound temporal position (cf., Fiebelkorn, Foxe, &amp; Molholm, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0016" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0016">2010</a>; Fiebelkorn, Foxe, Schwartz, &amp; Molholm, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0017" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0017">2010</a>); (b) separate paired <em>t</em> tests on ERP amplitudes between semantic congruent and incongruent conditions were conducted when sound preceded, synchronized and was delayed, respectively. To assess these follow‐up results with caution, <em>p</em> values for all follow‐up analyses in each direction above were further adjusted using the false discovery rate (FDR) correction (Benjamini &amp; Hochberg, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0003" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0003">1995</a>), and the FDR‐corrected <em>p</em> value was denoted as “<em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub>.” For completeness, both the uncorrected and FDR‐corrected <em>p</em> values were reported for each follow‐up analysis. Moreover, one‐sample <em>t</em> tests were conducted between 0 versus the mean amplitude of each ERP component in each of the six cross‐modal difference waveforms, respectively, to examine the presence/absence of each cross‐modal ERP in each VA condition. Again, both the uncorrected and FDR‐corrected <em>p</em> values were reported for each one‐sample <em>t</em> test.</p>
<p>Lastly, to further explore the relationship between the proposed psychophysiological processes indexed by the P195 and N440 components (for details, see section <a href="#hbm25797-sec-0013" class="usa-link">4</a>), the current study conducted two post hoc Pearson correlation analyses between the P195 and N440 amplitudes separately when a semantically incongruent sound synchronized with T2 and when a congruent sound synchronized with T2. The remaining four asynchronous‐sound conditions were not considered because neither of the two ERP components was statistically significant in any of the asynchronous‐sound conditions (see section <a href="#hbm25797-sec-0007" class="usa-link">3</a>). Both the uncorrected and FDR‐corrected <em>p</em> values were reported for each correlation coefficient.</p></section></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0007"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. RESULTS</h2>
<section id="hbm25797-sec-0008"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Behavioral data</h3>
<p>T2 discrimination accuracy (given correct T1 discrimination, the same below) was first compared between V_lag3 and V_lag8 conditions by a paired <em>t</em> test to check whether the basic visual attentional blink effect was successfully induced in the present study. Indeed, T2 accuracy was significantly lower at lag 3 than lag 8 [V_lag3: 43.0 ± 2.2% (<em>M</em> ± <em>SE</em>); V_lag8: 62.5 ± 2.8%; <em>t</em>(41) = −13.90, <em>p</em> &lt; .0001, Cohen's <em>d</em> = −2.14], indicative of a robust attentional blink effect (Raymond et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0054" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0054">1992</a>).</p>
<p>Next, within lag 3 trials, T2 accuracy in V_lag3 condition was subtracted from T2 accuracy in each VA condition (i.e., VAcon_prec, VAcon_sync, VAcon_delay, VAincon_prec, VAincon_sync, VAincon_delay) to obtain the “cross‐modal boost effect” (i.e., the beneficial effect of sound on T2 discrimination) in each of these conditions (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2a</a>). These difference measures were then subject to a 3 (sound temporal position: precede, synchronize, delay) × 2 (semantic congruency: congruent, incongruent) repeated‐measures ANOVA in order to examine the influences of audiovisual temporal synchrony and semantic congruency on the cross‐modal boost effect. The main effects of sound temporal position [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 6.05, <em>p</em> = .004, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.13] and semantic congruency [<em>F</em>(1,41) = 35.60, <em>p</em> &lt; .0001, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.47] were both significant. Importantly, the two‐way interaction was also significant [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 3.24, <em>p</em> = .044, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.07]. Further analysis of the interaction showed that the main effect of sound temporal position was nonsignificant in semantically incongruent conditions [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 0.26, <em>p</em> = .770, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.770, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.006; precede: −0.7 ± 0.8%; synchronize: −0.1 ± 0.9%; delay: −0.2 ± 0.9%], but was significant in semantically congruent conditions [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 8.84, <em>p</em> = .0003, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.0006, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.18]. Pairwise comparisons revealed that the cross‐modal boost effect was significantly larger when a congruent sound synchronized with T2 (4.7 ± 1.0%) than when that sound preceded T2 by 200 ms [1.2 ± 0.9%; <em>t</em>(41) = 4.13, <em>p</em> = .0002, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.0006, <em>d</em> = 0.64] and when it was delayed by 100 ms [1.9 ± 0.7%; <em>t</em>(41) = 3.16, <em>p</em> = .003, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.004, <em>d</em> = 0.49; Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2a</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="hbm25797-fig-0002"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/57aa/9057096/a80495f35dab/HBM-43-2478-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" id="jats-graphic-5" height="363" width="654" alt="FIGURE 2"></p>

<figcaption><p>Behavioral results. (a) Cross‐modal boost effect on lag 3 trials (quantified by the VA‐minus‐V difference in T2|T1 accuracy) as functions of sound temporal position (precede, synchronize, delay) and audiovisual semantic congruency (congruent, incongruent). The symbol “*” in white denotes a significant cross‐modal boost effect against zero. (b) Audiovisual semantic congruency effect on lag 3 trials (quantified by the congruent‐minus‐incongruent magnitude difference in cross‐modal boost effect) as a function of sound temporal position. In both graphs, the single‐subject data are depicted by gray scatter dots, the group‐averaged data are marked by black symbols, and error bars represent ±1 <em>SE</em>; *<em>p</em> &lt; .05; **<em>p</em> &lt; .01; ***<em>p</em> &lt; .001</p></figcaption></figure><p>Analyzing the two‐way interaction above in another direction found that, although the larger cross‐modal boost effect in semantically congruent than incongruent conditions occurred not only when sounds synchronized with T2s [<em>t</em>(41) = 5.25, <em>p</em> &lt; .0001, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> &lt; 0.0001, <em>d</em> = 0.81] but also when sounds preceded [<em>t</em>(41) = 2.04, <em>p</em> = .048, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.048, <em>d</em> = 0.31] and when sounds were delayed [<em>t</em>(41) = 2.38, <em>p</em> = .022, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.037, <em>d</em> = 0.37; Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2a</a>], the congruent‐minus‐incongruent magnitude difference in cross‐modal boost effect (i.e., the semantic congruency effect; see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2b</a>) was significantly greater when sounds synchronized with T2s (4.8 ± 0.9%) than when sounds preceded [1.8 ± 0.9%; <em>t</em>(41) = 2.47, <em>p</em> = .018, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.037, <em>d</em> = 0.38] and when sounds were delayed [2.1 ± 0.9%; <em>t</em>(41) = 2.06, <em>p</em> = .046, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.048, <em>d</em> = 0.32]. In addition, one‐sample <em>t</em> tests conducted between 0 versus the cross‐modal boost effect in each VA condition yielded that the cross‐modal boost effect was significant when a semantically congruent sound synchronized with T2 and when it was delayed relative to T2 by 100 ms [VAcon_sync: <em>t</em>(41) = 4.50, <em>p</em> &lt; .0001, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.0003, <em>d</em> = 0.69; VAcon_delay: <em>t</em>(41) = 2.50, <em>p</em> = .017, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.049, <em>d</em> = 0.39; Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2a</a>], but not in the remaining four VA conditions [VAcon_prec: <em>t</em>(41) = 1.27, <em>p</em> = .213, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.426, <em>d</em> = 0.19; VAincon_prec: <em>t</em>(41) = −0.87, <em>p</em> = .389, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.583, <em>d</em> = −0.13; VAincon_sync: <em>t</em>(41) = −0.07, <em>p</em> = .944, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.944, <em>d</em> = −0.01; VAincon_delay: <em>t</em>(41) = −0.17, <em>p</em> = .868, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.944, <em>d</em> = −0.03]. These findings indicates that (a) the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink originates from audiovisual cross‐modal interactions (which predicts the largest boost when a sound synchronizes with T2) rather than sound‐induced alerting effect (which predicts the largest boost when a sound precedes T2 by 200 ms), which is in agreement with Olivers and Van der Burg (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0052" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0052">2008</a>); (b) the semantic congruency effect on the cross‐modal boost during the attentional blink is modulated by audiovisual temporal synchrony, which is inconsistent with findings reported by Adam and Noppeney (<a href="#hbm25797-bib-0001" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0001">2014</a>).</p></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0009"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. 
ERP data</h3>
<p>Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3</a> displays the extracted ERP waveforms (time‐locked to lag 3 position) elicited by nondistractor stimuli in all but N condition [i.e., A_prec, A_sync, A_delay, V (V_lag3), VAcon_prec, VAcon_sync, VAcon_delay, VAincon_prec, VAincon_sync and VAincon_delay], which were obtained by subtracting the time‐locked ERPs in N condition from ERPs in each of the remaining 10 conditions (see section <a href="#hbm25797-sec-0006" class="usa-link">2.4</a>). As shown, the auditory‐only stimuli presented at lag 3 (A_sync) evoked classic auditory P1, N1 and P2 components with maxima over fronto‐central electrodes (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3a</a>, electrode FCz), and the auditory‐only stimuli presented 200 ms before and 100 ms after lag 3 position (A_prec and A_delay) elicited similar auditory ERPs but with a 200‐ms precedence and a 100‐ms delay in latency, respectively. The visual‐only T2s presented at lag 3 (V) elicited characteristic visual P1 and N1 components over bilateral parieto‐occipital electrodes (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3a</a>, electrode PO8). Among ERP waveforms elicited by the 6 types of bimodal VA stimuli, both the auditory and visual ERP components could be discerned, and the influence of audiovisual temporal asynchrony on the evolution of bimodal ERP waveforms could also be identified (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0003" class="usa-link">3b</a>). The patterns of these ERP waveforms confirm not only the precision of the manipulation of sound temporal position, but also the validity of introducing the N condition to cancel out distractor‐evoked ERPs in RSVP streams (Luo et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0038" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0038">2010</a>, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0039" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0039">2013</a>; Maier &amp; Rahman, <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0040" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0040">2018</a>; Sergent et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0056" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0056">2005</a>; Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="hbm25797-fig-0003"><h4 class="obj_head">FIGURE 3.</h4>

<figcaption><p>The grand‐averaged, <em>extracted</em> ERP waveforms (time‐locked to lag 3 position) elicited purely by non‐distractor stimuli in all but N condition, which were obtained by subtracting ERPs in N condition from ERPs in each of the remaining 10 conditions (see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0001" class="usa-link">1b</a> for reference). ERP waveforms elicited by unimodal V and A stimuli (a) and bimodal VA stimuli (b) are shown from electrodes FCz, PO8, P1 and O1. The electrodes FCz and PO8 were where auditory‐evoked ERP components (P1, N1 and P2) and visual‐evoked ERP components (P1 and N1) were largest, respectively, whereas the electrodes P1 and O1 were where the crucial cross‐modal N440 (424–448 ms) and P195 (192–228 ms) components were quantified, respectively</p></figcaption></figure><section id="hbm25797-sec-0010"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.1. Early cross‐modal P195 component</h4>
<p>To reveal neural activities underlying the cross‐modal boost of T2 discrimination during the attentional blink and examine the roles of audiovisual temporal synchrony and semantic congruency, audiovisual cross‐modal ERP components on the lag 3 trials when a semantically congruent or incongruent sound preceded T2, synchronized with T2, and was delayed relative to T2, were isolated respectively by calculating cross‐modal difference waveforms (see section <a href="#hbm25797-sec-0006" class="usa-link">2.4</a>). The occipitally distributed early cross‐modal negativity N195 (192–228 ms after T2 onset at the electrode O1), which has been shown to underlie the cross‐modal boost during the attentional blink (Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>), was manifested as a <em>positive</em> component in the present study (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0004" class="usa-link">4b</a>). Thus, this component was labeled as P195 in the present study for clarity. Then, a 3 (sound temporal position: precede, synchronize, delay) × 2 (semantic congruency: congruent, incongruent) repeated‐measures ANOVA was conducted on the mean amplitudes of P195 component. The results showed a significant main effect of sound temporal position [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 4.71, <em>p</em> = .012, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.10] and a nonsignificant main effect of semantic congruency [<em>F</em>(1,41) = 0.38, <em>p</em> = .543, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.01]. Importantly, there was a significant two‐way interaction [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 8.38, <em>p</em> = .0005, <em>η</em>

Furthermore, one‐sample <em>t</em> tests conducted between 0 versus the P195 amplitude in each of the six cross‐modal difference waveforms yielded that the P195 component was elicited significantly only when a semantically congruent sound synchronized with T2 [VAcon_sync: <em>t</em>(41) = 3.06, <em>p</em> = .004, <em>p</em>

These findings demonstrate that the occurrence of early cross‐modal P195 component is strongly contingent on the temporal synchrony between the sound and T2 and is further limited by audiovisual semantic incongruency, whose pattern is highly consistent with the pattern observed in the current behavioral results (see Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0002" class="usa-link">2a</a>). In terms of scalp topography, although the significant P195 in the VAcon_sync condition seemed to have both occipital and central distributions (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0004" class="usa-link">4b</a>), the central one was also prominent in the two preceding‐sound conditions (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0004" class="usa-link">4a</a>) wherein the occipital one was absent and no T2 accuracy improvement was observed. Therefore, the centrally distributed voltage was more likely to be unbalanced EEG noise or another cross‐modal interaction unrelated to the sound‐induced T2 accuracy improvement.</p></section><section id="hbm25797-sec-0011"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2. Late cross‐modal N440 component</h4>
<p>The late cross‐modal negativity N440 (424–448 ms after T2 onset at the electrode P1), which has been found to underlie the semantic congruency effect on the cross‐modal boost during the attentional blink (Zhao et al., <a href="#hbm25797-bib-0074" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="hbm25797-bib-0074">2021</a>), was also observed in the current study (Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5b</a>). This difference component appeared to be maximal in the temporally‐synchronous but semantically incongruent condition, wherein a parietal scalp distribution with a trend of left‐hemispheric preponderance could be observed. The same 3 (sound temporal position: precede, synchronize, delay) × 2 (semantic congruency: congruent, incongruent) repeated‐measures ANOVA conducted on the mean amplitudes of N440 component showed that neither the main effect of sound temporal position [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 2.23, <em>p</em> = .115, <em>η</em>
<sup>
<em>2</em>
</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.05] nor the main effect of semantic congruency [<em>F</em>(1,41) = 1.79, <em>p</em> = .188, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.04] reached significance, but the two‐way interaction was significant [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 4.75, <em>p</em> = .011, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.10]. Further analysis of the interaction revealed that the main effect of sound temporal position was not significant in semantically congruent conditions [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 0.43, <em>p</em> = .655, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.655, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>
<sub>
<em>p</em>
</sub> = 0.01; precede: −0.29 ± 0.26 μV; synchronize: −0.46 ± 0.27 μV; delay: −0.19 ± 0.29 μV], but was significant in semantically incongruent conditions [<em>F</em>(2,82) = 4.34, <em>p</em> = .016, <em>p</em>
<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.032, <em>η</em>
<sup>2</sup>

<sub>
<em>FDR</em>
</sub> = 0.156, <em>d</em> = −0.25; Figure <a href="#hbm25797-fig-0005" class="usa-link">5c</a>].</p>

<sub>

</sub> = 0.923, <em>d</em> = −0.01; VAcon_delay: <em>t</em>(41) = −0.67, <em>p</em> = .508, <em>p</em>

</sub> = 0.610, <em>d</em> = −0.10; VAincon_delay: <em>t</em>(41) = −1.64, <em>p</em> = .108, <em>p</em>
<sub>
